From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because... I just can’t stop seeing this sagging, uneven spot right around my jaw and my neck. It's just really bothering me. Even when folks tell me it looks normal, I just don't see it that way. I feel like it’s the very first thing the families I work with, or the nurses, or even people at church notice when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking or touching the area around your jaw and neck?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Especially before I leave for work or... or even just going out. I keep going back to the bathroom mirror to check it. I'll adjust my collar, try to fix my hair around my neck to hide it... and then I'll pull out my phone camera to take another look. I even catch myself asking my daughter if it looks strange. It's... well, it's actually made me late more than once because I just can't stop checking.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the appearance of your jaw and neck affected your relationships or work performance?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really has. At work... well, I’ve actually passed up a few chances to help lead meetings or take on some of the supervisory pieces. I just worry that if I'm up there talking, everyone will just be staring right at my face and my neck. And I’ve even fallen behind on my paperwork lately, because I get stuck trying to make myself look presentable instead of just sitting down and getting my tasks done. \n\nAnd outside of work... yeah. I’ve stopped going to some of my book club meetings, and I skip my walking club activities now, too. Just the thought of getting ready to see people feels like too much to deal with, so it's easier to just not go.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your jaw and neck that you're concerned about, or do you also worry about how other parts of your body look?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just my jaw and my neck. I mean... I don't really worry about the rest of my body. I know I'm getting older, and things change, but none of that really bothers me. It's just this one specific spot that I can't seem to stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your jaw and neck actually look uneven and sagging, or is it just that you feel like they should look different?\n\nPatient: I... I really do think it looks sagging and uneven. That's actually why I came in to see you. I can't stop seeing it, and it feels so obvious to me. Sometimes people tell me it looks normal, but I just don't see what they see. I feel like it's the very first thing families or nurses at work notice when they look at me. Or even people at church. It just feels so noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw and neck are actually defective in some way, or is it more that you're afraid they might look bad to others?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, I really do think there's an actual defect there. It's not just me being afraid of what people think. \n\nI just can't stop seeing this sagging, uneven spot around my jaw. That's really why I came in. Even when folks try to tell me it looks normal, I know what I see in the mirror. And because I see it so clearly, I just feel like it has to be the very first thing the families I care for, or the nurses at work, or the people at my church notice when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about the appearance of your jaw and neck if you were alone and never saw or interacted with other people?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I guess I've never really thought about it like that. I mean, I look in the mirror and I see it, so it bothers me regardless of who else is around. But, I suppose if I knew for a fact no one else would ever see me... maybe it wouldn't feel quite so heavy. I'm honestly not sure, though. I just know what I see when I look at myself.\n\nDoctor: Would your concern about your jaw and neck go away if you were convinced that it looked normal?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know. If I could actually see what other people say they see, and really believe it looked normal... maybe? But I just can't imagine that. Whenever I look in the mirror, the sagging is right there. It's so real to me. I don't know how I could ever be convinced it's normal when I can see with my own eyes that it's not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check or hide your jaw and neck if you were certain that others did not notice or care about its appearance?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I mean, if I knew for a fact that nobody else cared or noticed... I'd like to think I wouldn't feel the need to hide it so much. But honestly, I just don't know if I could stop checking it. Because even if they don't see it, I still do. It's still there when I look in the mirror, so I think I'd probably still check it just because it bothers me so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your jaw and neck would decrease if you were in a situation where you knew you would not be evaluated or judged by others?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say for sure. I mean, it might take the edge off a little bit if I knew nobody was judging me. But... I'm the one who has to look at it every day. Even if no one else is evaluating me, I'm still evaluating myself. So I think it would still bother me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your jaw and neck would be less bothersome if you could be certain that it was not noticeable to others, even if you still saw it as uneven yourself?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I mean... maybe it would take a little bit of the stress away, knowing for sure that people aren't staring at it. But I'd still know it's there. I'd still see the unevenness every time I looked in a mirror or caught my reflection. So I'm really not sure if it would actually bother me less at the end of the day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I mean, it might take the edge off a little bit if I knew nobody was judging me. But... I'm the one who has to look at it every day. Even if no one else is evaluating me, I'm still evaluating myself. So I think it would still bother me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your jaw and neck would be less bothersome if you could be certain that it was not noticeable to others, even if you still saw it as uneven yourself?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I mean... maybe it would take a little bit of the stress away, knowing for sure that people aren't staring at it. But I'd still know it's there. I'd still see the unevenness every time I looked in a mirror or caught my reflection. So I'm really not sure if it would actually bother me less at the end of the day.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your distress about your jaw and neck is more because of how you think they actually look or because of how you fear others might react to them?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 48 / personal_care_or_service_worker / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I honestly couldn't tell you which one it is more. It's... it's kind of just all rolled together for me. I really hate how it looks, just the actual shape and unevenness of it when I see it. But then, because I think it looks so bad, I constantly worry about what other people are thinking when they look at me. 

So I don't really know how to separate them to say which one causes more of the distress. I guess I'm just not sure.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because... I just can’t stop seeing this sagging, uneven spot right around my jaw and my neck. It's just really bothering me. Even when folks tell me it looks normal, I just don't see it that way. I feel like it’s the very first thing the families I work with, or the nurses, or even people at church notice when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking or touching the area around your jaw and neck?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Especially before I leave for work or... or even just going out. I keep going back to the bathroom mirror to check it. I'll adjust my collar, try to fix my hair around my neck to hide it... and then I'll pull out my phone camera to take another look. I even catch myself asking my daughter if it looks strange. It's... well, it's actually made me late more than once because I just can't stop checking.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the appearance of your jaw and neck affected your relationships or work performance?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really has. At work... well, I’ve actually passed up a few chances to help lead meetings or take on some of the supervisory pieces. I just worry that if I'm up there talking, everyone will just be staring right at my face and my neck. And I’ve even fallen behind on my paperwork lately, because I get stuck trying to make myself look presentable instead of just sitting down and getting my tasks done. \n\nAnd outside of work... yeah. I’ve stopped going to some of my book club meetings, and I skip my walking club activities now, too. Just the thought of getting ready to see people feels like too much to deal with, so it's easier to just not go.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your jaw and neck that you're concerned about, or do you also worry about how other parts of your body look?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just my jaw and my neck. I mean... I don't really worry about the rest of my body. I know I'm getting older, and things change, but none of that really bothers me. It's just this one specific spot that I can't seem to stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your jaw and neck actually look uneven and sagging, or is it just that you feel like they should look different?\n\nPatient: I... I really do think it looks sagging and uneven. That's actually why I came in to see you. I can't stop seeing it, and it feels so obvious to me. Sometimes people tell me it looks normal, but I just don't see what they see. I feel like it's the very first thing families or nurses at work notice when they look at me. Or even people at church. It just feels so noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw and neck are actually defective in some way, or is it more that you're afraid they might look bad to others?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, I really do think there's an actual defect there. It's not just me being afraid of what people think. \n\nI just can't stop seeing this sagging, uneven spot around my jaw. That's really why I came in. Even when folks try to tell me it looks normal, I know what I see in the mirror. And because I see it so clearly, I just feel like it has to be the very first thing the families I care for, or the nurses at work, or the people at my church notice when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about the appearance of your jaw and neck if you were alone and never saw or interacted with other people?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I guess I've never really thought about it like that. I mean, I look in the mirror and I see it, so it bothers me regardless of who else is around. But, I suppose if I knew for a fact no one else would ever see me... maybe it wouldn't feel quite so heavy. I'm honestly not sure, though. I just know what I see when I look at myself.\n\nDoctor: Would your concern about your jaw and neck go away if you were convinced that it looked normal?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know. If I could actually see what other people say they see, and really believe it looked normal... maybe? But I just can't imagine that. Whenever I look in the mirror, the sagging is right there. It's so real to me. I don't know how I could ever be convinced it's normal when I can see with my own eyes that it's not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check or hide your jaw and neck if you were certain that others did not notice or care about its appearance?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I mean, if I knew for a fact that nobody else cared or noticed... I'd like to think I wouldn't feel the need to hide it so much. But honestly, I just don't know if I could stop checking it. Because even if they don't see it, I still do. It's still there when I look in the mirror, so I think I'd probably still check it just because it bothers me so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your jaw and neck would decrease if you were in a situation where you knew you would not be evaluated or judged by others?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say for sure. I mean, it might take the edge off a little bit if I knew nobody was judging me. But... I'm the one who has to look at it every day. Even if no one else is evaluating me, I'm still evaluating myself. So I think it would still bother me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your jaw and neck would be less bothersome if you could be certain that it was not noticeable to others, even if you still saw it as uneven yourself?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I mean... maybe it would take a little bit of the stress away, knowing for sure that people aren't staring at it. But I'd still know it's there. I'd still see the unevenness every time I looked in a mirror or caught my reflection. So I'm really not sure if it would actually bother me less at the end of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your distress about your jaw and neck is more because of how you think they actually look or because of how you fear others might react to them?\n\nPatient: I honestly couldn't tell you which one it is more. It's... it's kind of just all rolled together for me. I really hate how it looks, just the actual shape and unevenness of it when I see it. But then, because I think it looks so bad, I constantly worry about what other people are thinking when they look at me. \n\nSo I don't really know how to separate them to say which one causes more of the distress. I guess I'm just not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. At work... well, I’ve actually passed up a few chances to help lead meetings or take on some of the supervisory pieces. I just worry that if I'm up there talking, everyone will just be staring right at my face and my neck. And I’ve even fallen behind on my paperwork lately, because I get stuck trying to make myself look presentable instead of just sitting down and getting my tasks done. \n\nAnd outside of work... yeah. I’ve stopped going to some of my book club meetings, and I skip my walking club activities now, too. Just the thought of getting ready to see people feels like too much to deal with, so it's easier to just not go.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your jaw and neck that you're concerned about, or do you also worry about how other parts of your body look?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just my jaw and my neck. I mean... I don't really worry about the rest of my body. I know I'm getting older, and things change, but none of that really bothers me. It's just this one specific spot that I can't seem to stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your jaw and neck actually look uneven and sagging, or is it just that you feel like they should look different?\n\nPatient: I... I really do think it looks sagging and uneven. That's actually why I came in to see you. I can't stop seeing it, and it feels so obvious to me. Sometimes people tell me it looks normal, but I just don't see what they see. I feel like it's the very first thing families or nurses at work notice when they look at me. Or even people at church. It just feels so noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw and neck are actually defective in some way, or is it more that you're afraid they might look bad to others?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, I really do think there's an actual defect there. It's not just me being afraid of what people think. \n\nI just can't stop seeing this sagging, uneven spot around my jaw. That's really why I came in. Even when folks try to tell me it looks normal, I know what I see in the mirror. And because I see it so clearly, I just feel like it has to be the very first thing the families I care for, or the nurses at work, or the people at my church notice when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about the appearance of your jaw and neck if you were alone and never saw or interacted with other people?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I guess I've never really thought about it like that. I mean, I look in the mirror and I see it, so it bothers me regardless of who else is around. But, I suppose if I knew for a fact no one else would ever see me... maybe it wouldn't feel quite so heavy. I'm honestly not sure, though. I just know what I see when I look at myself.\n\nDoctor: Would your concern about your jaw and neck go away if you were convinced that it looked normal?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know. If I could actually see what other people say they see, and really believe it looked normal... maybe? But I just can't imagine that. Whenever I look in the mirror, the sagging is right there. It's so real to me. I don't know how I could ever be convinced it's normal when I can see with my own eyes that it's not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check or hide your jaw and neck if you were certain that others did not notice or care about its appearance?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I mean, if I knew for a fact that nobody else cared or noticed... I'd like to think I wouldn't feel the need to hide it so much. But honestly, I just don't know if I could stop checking it. Because even if they don't see it, I still do. It's still there when I look in the mirror, so I think I'd probably still check it just because it bothers me so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your jaw and neck would decrease if you were in a situation where you knew you would not be evaluated or judged by others?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say for sure. I mean, it might take the edge off a little bit if I knew nobody was judging me. But... I'm the one who has to look at it every day. Even if no one else is evaluating me, I'm still evaluating myself. So I think it would still bother me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your jaw and neck would be less bothersome if you could be certain that it was not noticeable to others, even if you still saw it as uneven yourself?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I mean... maybe it would take a little bit of the stress away, knowing for sure that people aren't staring at it. But I'd still know it's there. I'd still see the unevenness every time I looked in a mirror or caught my reflection. So I'm really not sure if it would actually bother me less at the end of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your distress about your jaw and neck is more because of how you think they actually look or because of how you fear others might react to them?\n\nPatient: I honestly couldn't tell you which one it is more. It's... it's kind of just all rolled together for me. I really hate how it looks, just the actual shape and unevenness of it when I see it. But then, because I think it looks so bad, I constantly worry about what other people are thinking when they look at me. \n\nSo I don't really know how to separate them to say which one causes more of the distress. I guess I'm just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Would your distress about your jaw and neck decrease if you were certain that others did not view it as uneven or sagging, even if you still saw it that way yourself?\n\nPatient: I really don't know. I mean, it's hard to even imagine that, because to me it's just so obvious. But... if they really didn't see it? I guess I can't say for sure if I'd feel better. Because like I was saying before, I'd still be seeing the sagging and the unevenness myself. So I just don't know if my own distress would actually go down.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because... I just can’t stop seeing this sagging, uneven spot right around my jaw and my neck. It's just really bothering me. Even when folks tell me it looks normal, I just don't see it that way. I feel like it’s the very first thing the families I work with, or the nurses, or even people at church notice when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking or touching the area around your jaw and neck?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Especially before I leave for work or... or even just going out. I keep going back to the bathroom mirror to check it. I'll adjust my collar, try to fix my hair around my neck to hide it... and then I'll pull out my phone camera to take another look. I even catch myself asking my daughter if it looks strange. It's... well, it's actually made me late more than once because I just can't stop checking.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the appearance of your jaw and neck affected your relationships or work performance?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really has. At work... well, I’ve actually passed up a few chances to help lead meetings or take on some of the supervisory pieces. I just worry that if I'm up there talking, everyone will just be staring right at my face and my neck. And I’ve even fallen behind on my paperwork lately, because I get stuck trying to make myself look presentable instead of just sitting down and getting my tasks done. \n\nAnd outside of work... yeah. I’ve stopped going to some of my book club meetings, and I skip my walking club activities now, too. Just the thought of getting ready to see people feels like too much to deal with, so it's easier to just not go.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your jaw and neck that you're concerned about, or do you also worry about how other parts of your body look?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just my jaw and my neck. I mean... I don't really worry about the rest of my body. I know I'm getting older, and things change, but none of that really bothers me. It's just this one specific spot that I can't seem to stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your jaw and neck actually look uneven and sagging, or is it just that you feel like they should look different?\n\nPatient: I... I really do think it looks sagging and uneven. That's actually why I came in to see you. I can't stop seeing it, and it feels so obvious to me. Sometimes people tell me it looks normal, but I just don't see what they see. I feel like it's the very first thing families or nurses at work notice when they look at me. Or even people at church. It just feels so noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw and neck are actually defective in some way, or is it more that you're afraid they might look bad to others?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, I really do think there's an actual defect there. It's not just me being afraid of what people think. \n\nI just can't stop seeing this sagging, uneven spot around my jaw. That's really why I came in. Even when folks try to tell me it looks normal, I know what I see in the mirror. I guess I've never really thought about it like that. I mean, I look in the mirror and I see it, so it bothers me regardless of who else is around. But, I suppose if I knew for a fact no one else would ever see me... maybe it wouldn't feel quite so heavy. I'm honestly not sure, though. I just know what I see when I look at myself.\n\nDoctor: Would your concern about your jaw and neck go away if you were convinced that it looked normal?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know. If I could actually see what other people say they see, and really believe it looked normal... maybe? But I just can't imagine that. Whenever I look in the mirror, the sagging is right there. It's so real to me. I don't know how I could ever be convinced it's normal when I can see with my own eyes that it's not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check or hide your jaw and neck if you were certain that others did not notice or care about its appearance?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I mean, if I knew for a fact that nobody else cared or noticed... I'd like to think I wouldn't feel the need to hide it so much. But honestly, I just don't know if I could stop checking it. Because even if they don't see it, I still do. It's still there when I look in the mirror, so I think I'd probably still check it just because it bothers me so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your jaw and neck would decrease if you were in a situation where you knew you would not be evaluated or judged by others?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say for sure. I mean, it might take the edge off a little bit if I knew nobody was judging me. But... I'm the one who has to look at it every day. Even if no one else is evaluating me, I'm still evaluating myself. So I think it would still bother me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your jaw and neck would be less bothersome if you could be certain that it was not noticeable to others, even if you still saw it as uneven yourself?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I mean... maybe it would take a little bit of the stress away, knowing for sure that people aren't staring at it. But I'd still know it's there. I'd still see the unevenness every time I looked in a mirror or caught my reflection. So I'm really not sure if it would actually bother me less at the end of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your distress about your jaw and neck is more because of how you think they actually look or because of how you fear others might react to them?\n\nPatient: I honestly couldn't tell you which one it is more. It's... it's kind of just all rolled together for me. I really hate how it looks, just the actual shape and unevenness of it when I see it. But then, because I think it looks so bad, I constantly worry about what other people are thinking when they look at me. \n\nSo I don't really know how to separate them to say which one causes more of the distress. I guess I'm just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Would your distress about your jaw and neck decrease if you were certain that others did not view it as uneven or sagging, even if you still saw it that way yourself?\n\nPatient: I really don't know. Because like I was saying before, I'd still be seeing the sagging and the unevenness myself. I think I'm still where I was a minute ago with that one. It's just really hard to separate them. \n\nI just don't know which one actually causes more of the distress. They just kind of feed into each other, you know? Hating how it looks makes me worry about other people seeing it. So, yeah, I really couldn't pick one over the other. I just don't have a clear answer for that.\n\nDoctor: If you were completely alone and had no interactions with others, would your distress about the appearance of your jaw and neck still be as severe?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I work around people all day, so it's hard to even picture being totally alone like that. I mean... I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your distress about your jaw and neck would be less severe if you had no social interactions at all?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. Like I said, it's just hard to imagine not being around people since I'm always working with clients. I'd still see it myself, so I'd still be upset about it. But if it would be less severe? I just can't say for sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. And I’ve even fallen behind on my paperwork lately, because I get stuck trying to make myself look presentable instead of just sitting down and getting my tasks done. \n\nAnd outside of work... yeah. I’ve stopped going to some of my book club meetings, and I skip my walking club activities now, too. Just the thought of getting ready to see people feels like too much to deal with, so it's easier to just not go.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your jaw and neck that you're concerned about, or do you also worry about how other parts of your body look?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just my jaw and my neck. I mean... I don't really worry about the rest of my body. I know I'm getting older, and things change, but none of that really bothers me. It's just this one specific spot that I can't seem to stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your jaw and neck actually look uneven and sagging, or is it just that you feel like they should look different?\n\nPatient: I... I really do think it looks sagging and uneven. That's actually why I came in to see you. I can't stop seeing it, and it feels so obvious to me. Sometimes people tell me it looks normal, but I just don't see what they see. I feel like it's the very first thing families or nurses at work notice when they look at me. Or even people at church. It just feels so noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw and neck are actually defective in some way, or is it more that you're afraid they might look bad to others?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, I really do think there's an actual defect there. It's not just me being afraid of what people think. \n\nI just can't stop seeing this sagging, uneven spot around my jaw. That's really why I came in. Even when folks try to tell me it looks normal, I know what I see in the mirror. And because I see it so clearly, I just feel like it has to be the very first thing the families I care for, or the nurses at work, or the people at my church notice when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about the appearance of your jaw and neck if you were alone and never saw or interacted with other people?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I guess I've never really thought about it like that. I mean, I look in the mirror and I see it, so it bothers me regardless of who else is around. But, I suppose if I knew for a fact no one else would ever see me... maybe it wouldn't feel quite so heavy. I'm honestly not sure, though. I just know what I see when I look at myself.\n\nDoctor: Would your concern about your jaw and neck go away if you were convinced that it looked normal?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know. If I could actually see what other people say they see, and really believe it looked normal... maybe? But I just can't imagine that. Whenever I look in the mirror, the sagging is right there. It's so real to me. I don't know how I could ever be convinced it's normal when I can see with my own eyes that it's not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check or hide your jaw and neck if you were certain that others did not notice or care about its appearance?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I mean, if I knew for a fact that nobody else cared or noticed... I'd like to think I wouldn't feel the need to hide it so much. But honestly, I just don't know if I could stop checking it. Because even if they don't see it, I still do. It's still there when I look in the mirror, so I think I'd probably still check it just because it bothers me so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your jaw and neck would decrease if you were in a situation where you knew you would not be evaluated or judged by others?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say for sure. I mean, it might take the edge off a little bit if I knew nobody was judging me. But... I'm the one who has to look at it every day. Even if no one else is evaluating me, I'm still evaluating myself. So I think it would still bother me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your jaw and neck would be less bothersome if you could be certain that it was not noticeable to others, even if you still saw it as uneven yourself?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I mean... maybe it would take a little bit of the stress away, knowing for sure that people aren't staring at it. But I'd still know it's there. I'd still see the unevenness every time I looked in a mirror or caught my reflection. So I'm really not sure if it would actually bother me less at the end of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your distress about your jaw and neck is more because of how you think they actually look or because of how you fear others might react to them?\n\nPatient: I honestly couldn't tell you which one it is more. It's... it's kind of just all rolled together for me. I really hate how it looks, just the actual shape and unevenness of it when I see it. But then, because I think it looks so bad, I constantly worry about what other people are thinking when they look at me. \n\nSo I don't really know how to separate them to say which one causes more of the distress. I guess I'm just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Would your distress about your jaw and neck decrease if you were certain that others did not view it as uneven or sagging, even if you still saw it that way yourself?\n\nPatient: I really don't know. I mean, it's hard to even imagine that, because to me it's just so obvious. But... if they really didn't see it? I guess I can't say for sure if I'd feel better. Because like I was saying before, I'd still be seeing the sagging and the unevenness myself. So I just don't know if my own distress would actually go down.\n\nDoctor: Is your distress about your jaw and neck more related to the fact that you think it's actually uneven, or because you're worried about what others might think of it?\n\nPatient: Um... I think I'm still where I was a minute ago with that one. It's just really hard to separate them. \n\nI just don't know which one actually causes more of the distress. They just kind of feed into each other, you know? Hating how it looks makes me worry about other people seeing it. So, yeah, I really couldn't pick one over the other. I just don't have a clear answer for that.\n\nDoctor: If you were completely alone and had no interactions with others, would your distress about the appearance of your jaw and neck still be as severe?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I work around people all day, so it's hard to even picture being totally alone like that. I mean... I'd still have to look at myself in the mirror, so I know I'd still hate the way my neck and jaw look. But would it be exactly as bad as it is now, without anyone else around? I really couldn't tell you. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your distress about your jaw and neck would be less severe if you had no social interactions at all?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. Like I said, it's just hard to imagine not being around people since I'm always working with clients. I'd still see it myself, so I'd still be upset about it. But if it would be less severe? I just can't say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that being around other people makes your distress about your jaw and neck worse?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I mean, maybe? But since I'm pretty much always around people at my job, the stress is just kind of constant. I don't really know if being around them makes it spike or get worse, or if it's just because I'm always thinking about my jaw anyway. It's just hard to measure since the feeling is always there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I mean... I don't really worry about the rest of my body. I know I'm getting older, and things change, but none of that really bothers me. It's just this one specific spot that I can't seem to stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your jaw and neck actually look uneven and sagging, or is it just that you feel like they should look different?\n\nPatient: I... I really do think it looks sagging and uneven. That's actually why I came in to see you. I can't stop seeing it, and it feels so obvious to me. Sometimes people tell me it looks normal, but I just don't see what they see. I feel like it's the very first thing families or nurses at work notice when they look at me. Or even people at church. It just feels so noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw and neck are actually defective in some way, or is it more that you're afraid they might look bad to others?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, I really do think there's an actual defect there. It's not just me being afraid of what people think. \n\nI just can't stop seeing this sagging, uneven spot around my jaw. That's really why I came in. Even when folks try to tell me it looks normal, I know what I see in the mirror. And because I see it so clearly, I just feel like it has to be the very first thing the families I care for, or the nurses at work, or the people at my church notice when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about the appearance of your jaw and neck if you were alone and never saw or interacted with other people?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I guess I've never really thought about it like that. I mean, I look in the mirror and I see it, so it bothers me regardless of who else is around. But, I suppose if I knew for a fact no one else would ever see me... maybe it wouldn't feel quite so heavy. I'm honestly not sure, though. I just know what I see when I look at myself.\n\nDoctor: Would your concern about your jaw and neck go away if you were convinced that it looked normal?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know. If I could actually see what other people say they see, and really believe it looked normal... maybe? But I just can't imagine that. Whenever I look in the mirror, the sagging is right there. It's so real to me. I don't know how I could ever be convinced it's normal when I can see with my own eyes that it's not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check or hide your jaw and neck if you were certain that others did not notice or care about its appearance?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I mean, if I knew for a fact that nobody else cared or noticed... I'd like to think I wouldn't feel the need to hide it so much. But honestly, I just don't know if I could stop checking it. Because even if they don't see it, I still do. It's still there when I look in the mirror, so I think I'd probably still check it just because it bothers me so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your jaw and neck would decrease if you were in a situation where you knew you would not be evaluated or judged by others?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say for sure. I mean, it might take the edge off a little bit if I knew nobody was judging me. But... I'm the one who has to look at it every day. Even if no one else is evaluating me, I'm still evaluating myself. So I think it would still bother me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your jaw and neck would be less bothersome if you could be certain that it was not noticeable to others, even if you still saw it as uneven yourself?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I mean... maybe it would take a little bit of the stress away, knowing for sure that people aren't staring at it. But I'd still know it's there. I'd still see the unevenness every time I looked in a mirror or caught my reflection. So I'm really not sure if it would actually bother me less at the end of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your distress about your jaw and neck is more because of how you think they actually look or because of how you fear others might react to them?\n\nPatient: I honestly couldn't tell you which one it is more. It's... it's kind of just all rolled together for me. I really hate how it looks, just the actual shape and unevenness of it when I see it. But then, because I think it looks so bad, I constantly worry about what other people are thinking when they look at me. \n\nSo I don't really know how to separate them to say which one causes more of the distress. I guess I'm just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Would your distress about your jaw and neck decrease if you were certain that others did not view it as uneven or sagging, even if you still saw it that way yourself?\n\nPatient: I really don't know. I mean, it's hard to even imagine that, because to me it's just so obvious. But... if they really didn't see it? I guess I can't say for sure if I'd feel better. Because like I was saying before, I'd still be seeing the sagging and the unevenness myself. So I just don't know if my own distress would actually go down.\n\nDoctor: Is your distress about your jaw and neck more related to the fact that you think it's actually uneven, or because you're worried about what others might think of it?\n\nPatient: Um... I think I'm still where I was a minute ago with that one. It's just really hard to separate them. \n\nI just don't know which one actually causes more of the distress. They just kind of feed into each other, you know? Hating how it looks makes me worry about other people seeing it. So, yeah, I really couldn't pick one over the other. I just don't have a clear answer for that.\n\nDoctor: If you were completely alone and had no interactions with others, would your distress about the appearance of your jaw and neck still be as severe?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I work around people all day, so it's hard to even picture being totally alone like that. I mean... I'd still have to look at myself in the mirror, so I know I'd still hate the way my neck and jaw look. But would it be exactly as bad as it is now, without anyone else around? I really couldn't tell you. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your distress about your jaw and neck would be less severe if you had no social interactions at all?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. Like I said, it's just hard to imagine not being around people since I'm always working with clients. I'd still see it myself, so I'd still be upset about it. But if it would be less severe? I just can't say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that being around other people makes your distress about your jaw and neck worse?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I mean, maybe? But since I'm pretty much always around people at my job, the stress is just kind of constant. I don't really know if being around them makes it spike or get worse, or if it's just because I'm always thinking about my jaw anyway. It's just hard to measure since the feeling is always there.\n\nDoctor: Would your distress about your jaw and neck decrease if you were certain that it was not noticeable to others, but you still saw it as uneven yourself?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say. I mean, if I still saw it as uneven in the mirror, it would still bother me a lot. That's just how I see it. But if I knew for a fact no one else could see it... I don't know if that would actually make me feel any better or not. It's just really hard to separate the two things in my head.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. And I’ve even fallen behind on my paperwork lately, because I get stuck trying to make myself look presentable instead of just sitting down and getting my tasks done. \n\nAnd outside of work... yeah. I’ve stopped going to some of my book club meetings, and I skip my walking club activities now, too. Just the thought of getting ready to see people feels like too much to deal with, so it's easier to just not go.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your jaw and neck that you're concerned about, or do you also worry about how other parts of your body look?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just my jaw and my neck. I mean... I don't really worry about the rest of my body. I know I'm getting older, and things change, but none of that really bothers me. It's just this one specific spot that I can't seem to stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your jaw and neck actually look uneven and sagging, or is it just that you feel like they should look different?\n\nPatient: I... I really do think it looks sagging and uneven. That's actually why I came in to see you. I can't stop seeing it, and it feels so obvious to me. Sometimes people tell me it looks normal, but I just don't see what they see. I feel like it's the very first thing families or nurses at work notice when they look at me. Or even people at church. It just feels so noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw and neck are actually defective in some way, or is it more that you're afraid they might look bad to others?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a minute ago, I really do think there's an actual defect there. It's not just me being afraid of what people think. \n\nI just can't stop seeing this sagging, uneven spot around my jaw. That's really why I came in. Even when folks try to tell me it looks normal, I know what I see in the mirror. And because I see it so clearly, I just feel like it has to be the very first thing the families I care for, or the nurses at work, or the people at my church notice when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about the appearance of your jaw and neck if you were alone and never saw or interacted with other people?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I guess I've never really thought about it like that. I mean, I look in the mirror and I see it, so it bothers me regardless of who else is around. But, I suppose if I knew for a fact no one else would ever see me... maybe it wouldn't feel quite so heavy. I'm honestly not sure, though. I just know what I see when I look at myself.\n\nDoctor: Would your concern about your jaw and neck go away if you were convinced that it looked normal?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know. If I could actually see what other people say they see, and really believe it looked normal... maybe? But I just can't imagine that. Whenever I look in the mirror, the sagging is right there. It's so real to me. I don't know how I could ever be convinced it's normal when I can see with my own eyes that it's not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check or hide your jaw and neck if you were certain that others did not notice or care about its appearance?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I mean, if I knew for a fact that nobody else cared or noticed... I'd like to think I wouldn't feel the need to hide it so much. But honestly, I just don't know if I could stop checking it. Because even if they don't see it, I still do. It's still there when I look in the mirror, so I think I'd probably still check it just because it bothers me so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your jaw and neck would decrease if you were in a situation where you knew you would not be evaluated or judged by others?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say for sure. I mean, it might take the edge off a little bit if I knew nobody was judging me. But... I'm the one who has to look at it every day. Even if no one else is evaluating me, I'm still evaluating myself. So I think it would still bother me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about your jaw and neck would be less bothersome if you could be certain that it was not noticeable to others, even if you still saw it as uneven yourself?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I mean... maybe it would take a little bit of the stress away, knowing for sure that people aren't staring at it. But I'd still know it's there. I'd still see the unevenness every time I looked in a mirror or caught my reflection. So I'm really not sure if it would actually bother me less at the end of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your distress about your jaw and neck is more because of how you think they actually look or because of how you fear others might react to them?\n\nPatient: I honestly couldn't tell you which one it is more. It's... it's kind of just all rolled together for me. I really hate how it looks, just the actual shape and unevenness of it when I see it. But then, because I think it looks so bad, I constantly worry about what other people are thinking when they look at me. \n\nSo I don't really know how to separate them to say which one causes more of the distress. I guess I'm just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Would your distress about your jaw and neck decrease if you were certain that others did not view it as uneven or sagging, even if you still saw it that way yourself?\n\nPatient: I really don't know. I mean, it's hard to even imagine that, because to me it's just so obvious. But... if they really didn't see it? I guess I can't say for sure if I'd feel better. Because like I was saying before, I'd still be seeing the sagging and the unevenness myself. So I just don't know if my own distress would actually go down.\n\nDoctor: Is your distress about your jaw and neck more related to the fact that you think it's actually uneven, or because you're worried about what others might think of it?\n\nPatient: Um... I think I'm still where I was a minute ago with that one. It's just really hard to separate them. \n\nI just don't know which one actually causes more of the distress. They just kind of feed into each other, you know? Hating how it looks makes me worry about other people seeing it. So, yeah, I really couldn't pick one over the other. I just don't have a clear answer for that.\n\nDoctor: If you were completely alone and had no interactions with others, would your distress about the appearance of your jaw and neck still be as severe?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I work around people all day, so it's hard to even picture being totally alone like that. I mean... I'd still have to look at myself in the mirror, so I know I'd still hate the way my neck and jaw look. But would it be exactly as bad as it is now, without anyone else around? I really couldn't tell you. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your distress about your jaw and neck would be less severe if you had no social interactions at all?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. Like I said, it's just hard to imagine not being around people since I'm always working with clients. I'd still see it myself, so I'd still be upset about it. But if it would be less severe? I just can't say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that being around other people makes your distress about your jaw and neck worse?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I mean, maybe? But since I'm pretty much always around people at my job, the stress is just kind of constant. I don't really know if being around them makes it spike or get worse, or if it's just because I'm always thinking about my jaw anyway. It's just hard to measure since the feeling is always there.\n\nDoctor: Would your distress about your jaw and neck decrease if you were certain that it was not noticeable to others, but you still saw it as uneven yourself?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't say. I mean, if I still saw it as uneven in the mirror, it would still bother me a lot. That's just how I see it. But if I knew for a fact no one else could see it... I don't know if that would actually make me feel any better or not. It's just really hard to separate the two things in my head.\n\nDoctor: Is your distress about your jaw and neck more about the fact that you think it looks bad or because you believe others will judge you for it?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I think it's both, honestly. I hate how it looks when I see myself, but I also worry a lot about my clients noticing it when I'm working with them. I couldn't tell you which one is worse, though. It just all kind of blends together into one big source of stress for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
